The
\infty
-category of global sections
\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})\in\mathcal{P}r^{L}_{\operatorname{St}}
of
\mathcal{F}
is defined as the limit of
\mathcal{F}
.

•

The
\infty
-category of local sections
\mathcal{L}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})=\operatorname{Fun}_{\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})}(\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}),\Gamma(\mathcal{F}))\in\mathcal{P}r^{L}_{\operatorname{St}}
of
\mathcal{F}
is defined as the
\infty
-category of sections of the Grothendieck construction
p\colon\Gamma(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
.

Remark 3.11
.

As a model for the limit
\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})
of a
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober
\mathcal{F}
, we usually consider the full subcategory of the
\infty
-category of sections
\mathcal{L}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})
of the Grothendieck construction
p\colon\Gamma(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
of
\mathcal{F}
spanned by coCartesian sections. This description of the limit is proven in
[
Lur
, 7.4.1.10]
. A section
s\colon\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})\rightarrow\Gamma(\mathcal{F})
of
p
is called coCartesian if
s(\alpha)
is a coCartesian edge in the dual sense of
[
Lur09
, 2.4.1.1]
for each
1
-simplex
\alpha
in
\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober. Given an edge
e
of
{\bf G}
, we denote by
\operatorname{ev}_{e}\colon\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}(e)
the evaluation functor, which maps a coCartesian section
s
of
p
to its value
s(e)
at
e
. Consider the product of the evaluation functors at the external edges

\prod_{e\in{\bf G}_{1}^{\partial}}\operatorname{ev}_{e}\colon\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\prod_{e\in{\bf G}_{1}^{\partial}}\mathcal{F}(e)\,.

The functor
\prod_{e\in{\bf G}_{1}^{\partial}}\operatorname{ev}_{e}
preserves all limits and colimits by
[
Lur09
, 5.1.2.3, 4.3.1.10, 4.3.1.16]
. By the
\infty
-categorical adjoint functor theorem,
\prod_{e\in{\bf G}_{1}^{\partial}}\operatorname{ev}_{e}
thus admits a left adjoint.

Definition 3.12
.

The functor

\partial\mathcal{F}\colon\prod_{e\in{\bf G}_{1}^{\partial}}\mathcal{F}(e)\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F})

is defined as the left adjoint of
\prod_{e\in{\bf G}_{1}^{\partial}}\operatorname{ev}_{e}
.

Remark 3.13
.

In the study of partially wrapped Fukaya categories, particularly Fukaya-Seidel categories, the functor
\partial\mathcal{F}
is often called the Orlov functor or cup functor and
\prod_{e\in{\bf G}_{1}^{\partial}}\operatorname{ev}_{e}
is called the cap functor, see for instance
[
Syl19
]
for background.

3.3
Semiorthogonal decompositions of perverse schobers

Let
{\bf G}
be a ribbon graph. We note that a morphism
\alpha\colon\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
of
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schobers in one of the
\infty
-categories
\mathfrak{P}^{L}({\bf G})
or
\mathfrak{P}^{R}({\bf G})
, see
Definition

3.9
, is simply a natural transformation
\Delta^{1}\times\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}r^{L}_{\operatorname{St}}
or
\Delta^{1}\times\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}r^{R}_{\operatorname{St}}
between the diagrams defining
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
{\bf G}
be a ribbon graph and let
\alpha\colon\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
be a morphism of
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schobers in
\mathfrak{P}^{L}({\bf G})
or
\mathfrak{P}^{R}({\bf G})
.

1.

We call
\alpha
an inclusion of perverse schobers in
\mathfrak{P}^{L}({\bf G})
or
\mathfrak{P}^{R}({\bf G})
if
\alpha(x)\colon\mathcal{F}(x)\rightarrow\mathcal{G}(x)
is the inclusion of a stable subcategory (in particular fully faithful) for all
x\in\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
.

2.

Suppose that
\alpha
is an inclusion in
\mathfrak{P}^{L}({\bf G})
. We call
\alpha
right admissible if there exists a morphism
\beta\colon\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
in
\mathfrak{P}^{R}({\bf G})
such that
\beta(x)
is right adjoint to
\alpha(x)
for all
x\in\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
.

3.

Suppose that
\alpha
is an inclusion in
\mathfrak{P}^{R}({\bf G})
. We call
\alpha
left admissible if there exists a morphism
\beta\colon\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
in
\mathfrak{P}^{L}({\bf G})
such that
\beta(x)
is left adjoint to
\alpha(x)
for all
x\in\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
.

Remark 3.15
.

Let
\alpha\colon\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
be an inclusion of
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schobers. Spelling out the condition that
\alpha
is left admissible yields the requirement that for each morphism
v\rightarrow e
in
\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(v)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(v)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(v\rightarrow e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(v\rightarrow e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha(e)}

(8)

is left adjointable, meaning that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(v)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(v)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(v\rightarrow e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}(v\rightarrow e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta(e)}

commutes, with
\beta(x)
left adjoint to
\alpha(x)
for
x=v,e
. Analogously, the condition that
\alpha
is right admissible is equivalent to the right adjointability of the diagram (
8
). Adjointability of a commutative square is also called the
Beck-Chevalley condition
.

Definition 3.16
.

Let
{\bf G}
be a ribbon graph. A semiorthogonal decomposition
\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}\}
of a
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober
\mathcal{G}
consists of

•

an inclusion
\alpha_{1}\colon\mathcal{F}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
in
\mathfrak{P}^{R}({\bf G})
and

•

an inclusion
\alpha_{2}\colon\mathcal{F}_{2}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}
in
\mathfrak{P}^{L}({\bf G})
, satisfying that

•

\alpha_{2}
is right admissible and that

•

the cofiber of
\alpha_{2}
in
\operatorname{Fun}(\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}),\mathcal{P}r^{L}_{\operatorname{St}})
is given by
\mathcal{F}_{1}
, with cofiber morphism
\beta\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{F}_{1}
pointwise left adjoint to
\alpha_{1}
, thus exhibiting
\alpha_{1}
as left admissible.

Remark 3.17
.

Consider a semiorthogonal decomposition
\{\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathcal{F}_{2}\}
of the
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober
\mathcal{G}
.

1.

Then for each
x\in\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G})
, the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{G}(x)
admits a semiorthogonal decomposition
(\mathcal{F}_{1}(x),\mathcal{F}_{2}(x))
.

2.

The condition that
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is the cofiber of
\alpha_{2}
in
\operatorname{Fun}(\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}),\mathcal{P}r^{L}_{\operatorname{St}})
is equivalent to the condition that
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is the fiber of the pointwise right adjoint
\beta_{1}\colon\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{1}
of
\alpha_{1}
in
\operatorname{Fun}(\operatorname{Exit}({\bf G}),\mathcal{P}r^{R}_{\operatorname{St}})
. This follows from the fact that limits and colimits in functor categories are computed pointwise, see
[
Lur09
, 5.1.2.3]
. Since limits commute with limits, we find that there exists a fiber sequence in
\mathcal{P}r^{L}_{\operatorname{St}}

\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F}_{2})\xrightarrow{i}\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{G})\xrightarrow{\pi}\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F}_{1})\,.

Passing to right adjoints yields a cofiber sequence in
\mathcal{P}^{R}_{\operatorname{St}}
, showing by
Lemma

2.7
that
\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{G})
admits a semiorthogonal decomposition

\big(\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F}_{1}),\mathcal{H}({\bf G},\mathcal{F}_{2})\big)\,.

Definition 3.18
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a
{\bf G}
-parametrized perverse schober. Recall that given a vertex
v
of
{\bf G}
,
\mathcal{F}
is described near
v
by a spherical functor
F\colon\mathcal{V}_{v}\to\mathcal{N}
, where
\mathcal{V}_{v}
is called the
\infty
-category of vanishing cycles and
\mathcal{N}
the
\infty
-category of nearby cycles. We call

•

\mathcal{F}
vanishing-monadic, if at each vertex
v
of
{\bf G}
the spherical functor
F_{v}\colon\mathcal{V}_{v}\rightarrow\mathcal{N}
describing
\mathcal{F}
at
v
is a monadic functor.

•

\mathcal{F}
nearby-monadic, if at each vertex
v
of
{\bf G}
the right adjoint of the spherical functor
F_{v}
describing
\mathcal{F}
at
v
is a monadic functor.

•

\mathcal{F}
locally constant if
\mathcal{F}
has no singularities.

Remark 3.19
.

A spherical functor between presentable
\infty
-categories is monadic if and only if it is comonadic if and only if it is conservative, as follows from the Lurie–Barr–Beck theorem
[
Lur17
, 4.7.3.5]
.

4
Generalized cluster categories of surfaces and perverse schobers

We fix a field
k
and a marked surface
{\bf S}
with a trivalent spanning graph
\mathcal{T}
. Theorem 6.1 in
[
Chr22a
]
constructs a
\mathcal{T}
-parametrized perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
, whose
\infty
-category of global sections
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})
is equivalent to the unbounded derived
\infty
-category of the relative Ginzburg algebra
\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}}
associated with
\mathcal{T}
. The potential of the underlying quiver consists of
3
-cycles, one for each triangle of the dual ideal triangulation of
\mathcal{T}
. In
Sections

4.1
and
4.2
, we describe the generalized cluster category associated to
\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}}
in terms of the global sections of a component of a semiorthogonal decomposition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
. Using this, we are able to deduce in
Section

4.3
that the generalized cluster category is equivalent to the
1
-periodic topological Fukaya category.

4.1
A semiorthogonal decomposition of perverse schobers

Consider the
\mathcal{T}
-parametrized perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
from
[
Chr22a
, Theorem 6.1]
. The spherical adjunction underlying
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
at any vertex of
\mathcal{T}
is given by

f^{*}\colon\mathcal{D}(k)\longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))\penalty\mspace{6muplus1mu}{:}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu\mathpunct{}\mspace{2mu}f_{*}

where
f^{*}
is the pullback functor along
S^{2}\rightarrow\ast
. There is a
k
-linear equivalence
\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))\simeq\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}])
, where
k[t_{1}]
is the graded polynomial algebra with
|t_{1}|=1
, under which the functor
f^{*}
is identified with the pullback functor
\phi^{*}
along
\phi\colon k[t_{1}]\xrightarrow{t_{1}\mapsto 0}k
, see
[
Chr22a
, Prop. 5.5]
. In particular, the functor
f^{*}
is conservative and the adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
thus comonadic. The adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
is however not monadic. The Eilenberg-Moore
\infty
-category
\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))^{\operatorname{mnd}}
of the monad
f_{*}f^{*}
can be identified with the full subcategory
\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k))\subset\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))
, see
Lemma

2.10
. Using this observation, we can obtain a nearby-monadic and vanishing-monadic
\mathcal{T}
-parametrized perverse schober
\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{mnd}}_{\mathcal{T}}
by restricting in the construction of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
in
[
Chr21
]
the spherical adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
to the spherical monadic and comonadic adjunction

(f^{*})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}\colon\mathcal{D}(k)\longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k))\penalty\mspace{6muplus1mu}{:}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu\mathpunct{}\mspace{2mu}f_{*}^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}\,.

We remark that to give this definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{mnd}}
, we also use that in the construction of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
all appearing autoequivalences of
\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))
preserve the subcategory
\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k))\subset\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))
.

There is an apparent inclusion of perverse schobers
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{mnd}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

The inclusion
\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{mnd}}_{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
is right admissible.

Proof.

By
Remark

3.15
, we need to show that for
1\leq i\leq 3
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}^{3}_{(f^{*})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}^{3}_{f^{*}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\mathcal{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\operatorname{mnd}}}

(9)

is right adjointable. We only consider the case
i=1
, the other cases can be treated analogously or also follow using
[
Chr22a
, Prop. 3.11]
from the
i=1
case.

It is straightforward to see that the following diagram commutes,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{perf}}(k))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{*})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f^{*})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\operatorname{mnd}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\operatorname{mnd}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{\operatorname{mnd}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\operatorname{mnd}}}

where
\pi_{\operatorname{mnd}}
denotes the right adjoint of
i_{\operatorname{mnd}}
. The right adjoint of the functor
i_{\mathcal{V}}
between lax limits is obtained by passing componentwise to the right adjoint, see
[
CDW23
, Remark A.2.5]
, and thus acts on the third component via
\pi_{\operatorname{mnd}}
. This shows the desired right adjointability.
∎

Definition 4.2
.

We define the functor
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}\colon\operatorname{Exit}(\mathcal{T})\rightarrow\operatorname{St}
as the cofiber of the inclusion
\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{mnd}}_{\mathcal{T}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
in
\operatorname{Fun}(\operatorname{Exit}({\mathcal{T}}),\operatorname{St})
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The functor
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
is a
\mathcal{T}
-parametrized perverse schober. At each vertex of
\mathcal{T}
, it is described by the trivial spherical adjunction

0\leftrightarrow\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])\,.

Proof.

Using
Lemma

2.11
, this is straightforward to check by using that pushouts in the functor category
\operatorname{Fun}(\operatorname{Exit}(\mathcal{T}),\operatorname{St})
are computed pointwise in
\operatorname{Exit}(\mathcal{T})
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

The abbreviation
\operatorname{clst}
stands for ’cluster’, or rearranging the letters to
\operatorname{lcst}
, it stands for ’locally constant’. Note that the perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{{\bf G}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
is locally constant in the sense of
Definition

3.18
, meaning that
\mathcal{F}_{{\bf G}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
has no singularities.

Proposition 4.5
.

The pair
\{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{mnd}}_{\mathcal{T}}\}
forms a semiorthogonal decomposition of the
\mathcal{T}
-parametrized perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
.

Proof.

The inclusion
\alpha\colon\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{mnd}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
is by
Lemma

4.1
right admissible. A similar argument as in the proof of
Lemma

4.1
further shows that the cofiber morphism
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
arising from the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
is the pointwise left adjoint of a left admissible inclusion
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
. This shows that
\{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}},\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{mnd}}_{\mathcal{T}}\}
indeed forms a semiorthogonal decomposition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
.
∎

4.2
The generalized cluster category of a marked surface

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be a compactly generated
k
-linear
\infty
-category.

(1)

We define the finite subcategory
\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{fin}}\subset\mathcal{D}
as the full subcategory consisting of objects
Y\in\mathcal{D}
, satisfying that
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(X,Y)\in\mathcal{D}(k)
is perfect for all compact objects
X\in\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{c}}
. We note if
\mathcal{D}
admits a compact generator
Z
, then
Y
is finite if and only if
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}}(Z,Y)
is perfect.

(2)

Suppose that
\mathcal{D}
is smooth. Then the evaluation functor of
\mathcal{D}
admits a left adjoint, which we identify with an endofunctor
\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}^{!}
of
\mathcal{D}
(also known as the inverse dualizing bimodule). Suppose further that
\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{D}}^{!}
is an equivalence. Then the finite objects in
\mathcal{D}
are compact, i.e.
\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{fin}}\subset\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{c}}
, see
[
Chr23
, Lem. 2.23]
. Passing to
\operatorname{Ind}
-completions yields the fully faithful inclusion
\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{fin}}\subset\mathcal{D}
. We define the generalized cluster category of
\mathcal{D}
as the
\operatorname{Ind}
-complete Verdier quotient
\mathcal{D}/\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{fin}}
(i.e. cofiber in
\mathcal{P}r^{L}_{\operatorname{St}}
).

Remark 4.7
.

Let
\mathcal{D}
be as in part (2) of
Definition

4.6
. Then
\mathcal{D}
admits a semiorthogonal decomposition
(\mathcal{D}/\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{fin}},\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{D}^{\operatorname{fin}})
into its generalized cluster category and its
\operatorname{Ind}
-finite part, see
Lemma

2.7
.

The goal of this section is to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 4.8
.

The generalized cluster category of
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})
is equivalent to the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}})
of global sections of the perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
.

Remark 4.9
.

A shown in
[
Chr22a
, Section 6.4]
, the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})\simeq\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})
does not depend on the choice of trivalent spanning graph
\mathcal{T}
of the marked surface
{\bf S}
, up to equivalence of
\infty
-categories. The same thus also holds for its associated generalized cluster category. We therefore write
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}})
and call
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}
the generalized cluster category of the marked surface
{\bf S}
.

To prove
Theorem

4.8
, we show that the
\infty
-category of global sections of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{mnd}}
is equivalent to
\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{fin}}
and then make use of the semiorthogonal decomposition
\{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{mnd}}\}
of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
. The argument for the former relies on a computational argument using compact generators arising from curves described in
[
Chr21
]
.

Definition 4.10
.

We denote by
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}\subset\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})
the full subcategory of global sections
X\in\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})
satisfying that
\operatorname{ev}_{e}(X)\in\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k)^{\operatorname{perf}})
for all edges
e
of
\mathcal{T}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}
is compactly generated by the objects
M_{\gamma}^{L}
associated to finite matching data
(\gamma,f^{*}(k))
with
\gamma
pure and
f^{*}(k)\in\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))^{\operatorname{fin}}\subset\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))
, as defined in
[
Chr21
]
.

Proof.

Using that
f^{*}(k)
is a compact generator of
\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))^{\operatorname{fin}}
,
[
Chr21
, Prop. 5.19]
shows that a certain collection of maching data with local value
f^{*}(k)
and pure, finite underlying matching curves compactly generate
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}
. We remark that for the fact that the matching curves giving rise to this compact generator are finite, it is crucial that the marked surface has a marked point on each boundary component.
∎

Lemma 4.12
.

For
e
an edge of
\mathcal{T}
, consider the left adjoint

\operatorname{ev}^{L}_{e}\colon\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}])\simeq\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}(e)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})

of the evaluation functor
\operatorname{ev}_{e}
at
e
. The object
\bigoplus_{e\in\mathcal{T}_{1}}\operatorname{ev}^{L}_{e}(k[t_{1}])\in\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})
is identified under the equivalence
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})\simeq\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})
with the relative Ginzburg algebra
\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})
. In particular,
\bigoplus_{e\in\mathcal{T}_{1}}\operatorname{ev}^{L}_{e}(k[t_{1}])
is a compact generator.

Proof.

This is shown in
[
Chr22a
, Prop. 6.7]
.
∎

Proposition 4.13
.

There exist equivalences of
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{mnd}})\simeq\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}\simeq\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{fin}}\,.

Proof.

It follows from the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{mnd}}
, that the image on global sections of the inclusion
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{mnd}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}
consists of global sections which evaluate on each edge of
\mathcal{T}
to an object in
\operatorname{Ind}\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k)^{\operatorname{perf}})\subset\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))
. We thus see that
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{mnd}})\simeq\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}
.

We proceed with showing the equivalence
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}\simeq\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{fin}}
. We can realize both of these
\infty
-categories as presentable, stable subcategories of
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})
. An object of
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})
is by
Lemma

4.12
finite if and only if its evaluations to the edges of
\mathcal{T}
are finite in
\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))
, which is equivalent to lying in
\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k)^{\operatorname{perf}})\subset\operatorname{Fun}(S^{2},\mathcal{D}(k))
. It follows that
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{fin}}\subset\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}
. Using that the objects in
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{fin}}
are compact in
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})
, we further get
\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{fin}}\subset\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}
.
Lemma

4.11
, in combination with the fact that the global sections associated to finite, pure matching data with finite local value, in the sense of
[
Chr21
]
, lie by construction in
\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{fin}}\subset\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}
, now implies that both
\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{Ind-fin}}
and
\operatorname{Ind}\mathcal{D}(\mathscr{G}_{\mathcal{T}})^{\operatorname{fin}}
are compactly generated by the same set of objects and thus equivalent.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.8
.

Combine
Proposition

4.5
,
Remark

3.17
.2 and
Proposition

4.13
.
∎

The dg-algebra
k[t_{2}^{\pm}]
is commutative and the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
comes with an apparent
k[t_{2}^{\pm}]
-linear structure. This can be used to construct a factorization of the perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
through
\operatorname{LinCat}_{k[t_{2}^{\pm}]}\rightarrow\operatorname{St}
. In particular, its limit
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}=\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}})
inherits a
k[t_{2}^{\pm}]
-linear structure.

Proposition 4.14
.

The
k[t_{2}^{\pm}]
-linear
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}
is smooth and proper.

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}
is equivalent to the colimit of the right adjoint diagram of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
in
\operatorname{LinCat}_{k[t_{2}^{\pm}]}
. The smoothness thus follows from the fact that finite colimits of smooth
k[t_{2}^{\pm}]
-linear
\infty
-categories along compact objects preserving functors are again smooth, see
[
Chr23
, Cor. 3.11]
.

To see that
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}
is proper, consider the compact generator
\bigoplus_{e\in\mathcal{T}_{1}}\operatorname{ev}^{L}_{e}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
of
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}
, given by the sum of the images of the left adjoints of the evaluation functors
\operatorname{ev}_{e}\colon\mathcal{H}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}(e)\simeq\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
. Proposition 5.19 in
[
Chr21
]
shows that
\operatorname{ev}^{L}_{e}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])\simeq M_{c_{e}}^{k[t_{1}^{\pm}]}
for some matching datum
(c_{e},k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
, where
c_{e}
is a finite, pure matching curve (or equivalently
\operatorname{ev}^{L}_{e}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])\simeq M_{c_{e}}
in the notation of
Section

6.2
below for some matching curve
c_{e}
). Using
Theorems

6.7
and
6.8
, we see that
\operatorname{End}(\bigoplus_{e\in\mathcal{T}_{1}}\operatorname{ev}^{L}_{e}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}]))
is perfect in
\mathcal{D}(k[t_{2}^{\pm}])
, showing that
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}
is proper.
∎

4.3
Relation to the 1-periodic topological Fukaya category

The perverse schober
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
is locally constant, i.e. has no singularities, and its generic stalk is the derived
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
of
1
-periodic chain complexes. It is thus locally described by the
1
-periodic derived category of the
A_{2}
-quiver. Global sections of locally constant perverse schobers may be referred to as topological Fukaya categories, as these recover in special cases the constructions given in
[
DK18
,
DK15
,
HKK17
]
. We refer to the
\infty
-category of global sections
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}=\mathcal{H}({\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}})
as the topological Fukaya category of
{\bf S}
with values in the derived
\infty
-category of
1
-periodic chain complexes, or the
1
-periodic topological Fukaya category for short.

There is a notion of monodromy for parametrized perverse schobers
[
Chr23
]
and this monodromy of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}
along any closed curve in
{\bf S}^{\circ}
is trivial, this is shown in the proof of Theorem 6.5 in
[
Chr21
]
. Furthermore, any locally constant parametrized perverse schober is fully determined up to equivalence by its generic stalk and monodromy, see
[
Chr23
, Prop. 4.26]
. We are hence justified in calling the generalized cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}

the

1
-periodic topological Fukaya category of
{\bf S}
.

The
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}^{\operatorname{c}}
can further be considered as an
\infty
-categorical avatar of the topological Fukaya (dg-)category in the sense of
[
DK18
]
with coefficients in the cyclic
2
-Segal object arising from the Waldhausen
S_{\bullet}
-construction of the
2
-periodic dg-category
\operatorname{dgMod}_{k[t_{1}^{\pm}]}
of dg
k[t_{1}^{\pm}]
-modules. In particular, Corollary 3.4.7 of
[
DK18
]
shows the following.

Theorem 4.15
.

The stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}
is acted upon by automorphisms in
\operatorname{ho}\operatorname{LinCat}_{k}
by the mapping class group of the surface
{\bf S}
of isotopy classes of orientation preserving diffeomorphisms
{\bf S}\rightarrow{\bf S}
restricting to the identity on
\partial{\bf S}
.

Finally, we also record a description of the generalized cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}
in terms of a gentle algebra.

Proposition 4.16
.

There exists an ungraded gentle algebra
\operatorname{gtl}
and an equivalence of
k
-linear
\infty
-categories
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}\simeq\mathcal{D}(\operatorname{gtl})\otimes_{k}\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
.

Proof.

Choose a trivalent spanning graph
\mathcal{T}
of
{\bf S}
. As observed in
[
HKK17
]
, the
\infty
-category of global sections of a locally constant perverse schober with generic stalk
\mathcal{D}(k)
on
{\bf S}
parametrized by
\mathcal{T}
admits a formal generator whose endomorphism algebra is a finite dimensional, and in general graded, gentle algebra, denoted
\operatorname{gtl}^{\prime}
. Using that tensoring with
\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
(with respect to the symmetric monoidal structure of
\operatorname{LinCat}_{k}
) preserves colimits in
\operatorname{LinCat}_{k}
, we find an equivalence
\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}\simeq\mathcal{D}(\operatorname{gtl}^{\prime})\otimes_{k}\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
in
\operatorname{LinCat}_{k}
. Let
\operatorname{gtl}
be the ungraded gentle algebra obtained from
\operatorname{gtl}^{\prime}
by discarding the grading. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{D}(\operatorname{gtl}^{\prime})\otimes_{k}\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])\simeq\mathcal{D}(\operatorname{gtl})\otimes_{k}\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
in
\operatorname{LinCat}_{k}
, showing the claim.
∎

Topological Fukaya categories admit relative Calabi–Yau structures if the monodromy of the perverse schober acts trivially on a relative Calabi–Yau structure of the generic stalk, see
[
Chr23
, Thm. 5.11]
. In our setting, this gives the following.

Theorem 4.17

(
[
Chr23
, Thm. 6.1]
)
.

Assume that
\operatorname{char}(k)\neq 2
. The
k[t_{2}^{\pm}]
-linear functor

\prod_{e\in\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\partial}}\operatorname{ev}_{e}\colon\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}\longrightarrow\prod_{e\in\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\partial}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}(e)

admits a
2
-Calabi–Yau structure in the sense of
Definition

2.14
.

Corollary 4.18
.

Assume that
\operatorname{char}(k)\neq 2
. The adjunction

\partial\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}\colon\prod_{e\in\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\partial}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}(e)\longleftrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}\penalty\mspace{6muplus1mu}{:}\nonscript\mkern-3.0mu\mathpunct{}\mspace{2mu}\prod_{e\in\mathcal{T}_{1}^{\partial}}\operatorname{ev}_{e}

is spherical.

Proof.

The twist functor of the adjunction
\partial\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{clst}}_{{\bf G}}\dashv\prod_{e\in{\bf G}_{1}^{\partial}}\operatorname{ev}_{e}
is by
Theorem

4.17
a suspension of the inverse Serre functor
\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{C}_{\bf S}}^{!}
and thus invertible. The cotwist functor of the adjunction
\partial\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{clst}}_{{\bf G}}\dashv\prod_{e\in{\bf G}_{1}^{\partial}}\operatorname{ev}_{e}
can be readily determined using the equivalence
\operatorname{ev}^{L}_{e}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])\simeq M_{c_{e}}^{k[t_{1}^{\pm}]}
from the proof of
Proposition

4.14
, and shown to be an equivalence. It acts by permuting cyclically the copies of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{T}}^{\operatorname{clst}}(e)\simeq\mathcal{D}(k[t_{1}^{\pm}])
corresponding to the external edges of each boundary circle.
∎

5
Exact structures from relative Calabi–Yau structures

Consider an exact functor
G\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
between stable
\infty
-categories, meaning a functor which preserves finite limits and colimits. We can pull back the split-exact structure on
\mathcal{A}
to an exact structure on the
\infty
-category
\mathcal{B}
, such that a sequence in
\mathcal{B}
is exact if and only if its image under
G
is a split-exact sequence in
\mathcal{A}
, see
Example

5.8
. The goal of this section is to show that if
G
carries a right
2
-Calabi–Yau structure and is spherical, then the exact structure on
\mathcal{B}
is Frobenius and its extriangulated homotopy category is
2
-Calabi–Yau.

In
Section

5.1
, we recall the notion of an exact
\infty
-category and describe pullbacks of exact structures. In
Section

5.2
, we recall what is an extriangulated category and why it is natural to consider the extriangulated homotopy categories of exact
\infty
-categories. In
Section

5.3
, we prove that the Frobenius extriangulated structure arising from a functor carrying a right
2
-Calabi–Yau structure is extriangulated
2
-Calabi–Yau.

5.1
Exact
\infty
-categories

Definition 5.1

(
[
Bar15
]
)
.

An exact
\infty
-category is a triple
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
, where
\mathcal{C}
is an additive
\infty
-category and
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}\subset\mathcal{C}
are subcategories (called subcategories of inflations and deflations), satisfying that

(1)

every morphism
0\rightarrow X
in
\mathcal{C}
lies in
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
and every morphism
X\rightarrow 0
in
\mathcal{C}
lies in
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
.

(2)

pushouts in
\mathcal{C}
along morphisms in
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
exist and lie in
\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
. Dually, pullbacks in
\mathcal{C}
along morphisms in
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
exist and lie in
\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
.

(3)

Given a commutative square in
\mathcal{C}
of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

the following are equivalent.

•

The square is pullback,
c\in\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
and
d\in\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
.

•

The square is pushout,
b\in\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
and
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
.

When the exact structure is clear from the context, we also simply refer to
\mathcal{C}
as an exact
\infty
-category.

Definition 5.2
.

An exact sequence
X\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z
in an exact
\infty
-category
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
consists of a fiber and cofiber sequence in
\mathcal{C}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

with
a\in\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
and
b\in\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
.

Example 5.3
.

1.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an additive
\infty
-category. Then there is an exact
\infty
-category
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
with inflations consisting of inclusions of direct summands and deflations consisting of projections onto direct summands, called the split-exact structure. The exact sequences are the split-exact sequences
X\hookrightarrow X\oplus Y\twoheadrightarrow Y
.

2.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category. Then
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
is an exact
\infty
-category.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
be an exact
\infty
-category.

1)

An object
P\in\mathcal{C}
is called projective if every exact sequence
X\rightarrow Y\rightarrow P
is split-exact. An object
I\in\mathcal{C}
is called injective if every exact sequence
I\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z
is split-exact.

2)

We say that
\mathcal{C}
has enough projectives if for each object
X\in\mathcal{C}
there exists an exact sequence
X\rightarrow P\rightarrow Y
with
P
projective. Similarly, we say that
\mathcal{C}
has enough injectives if for each object
Y\in\mathcal{C}
there exists an exact sequence
Y\rightarrow I\rightarrow X
with
I
injective.

3)

We say that
\mathcal{C}
is Frobenius if
\mathcal{C}
has enough projectives and injectives and the classes of projective and injective objects coincide.

The
\infty
-categorical version of the stable
1
-category of a Frobenius exact
1
-category is the following.

Proposition 5.5

(
[
JKPW22
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
be a Frobenius exact
\infty
-category and
W
the class of morphisms
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
which fit into an exact sequence

X\xlongrightarrow{(f,g)}Y\oplus I\longrightarrow J

with
I
and
J
injective and
g
arbitrary. Then, the
\infty
-categorical localisation
\bar{\mathcal{C}}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}[W^{-1}]
of
\mathcal{C}
at
W
is a stable
\infty
-category.

Proof idea.

Applying
[
Cis19
, Prop. 7.5.6]
and its dual version, one finds that
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
admits finite limits and colimits and pushouts and pullbacks coincide.
∎

Definition 5.6
.

1.

An exact functor
G\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
between exact
\infty
-categories consists of a functor
G\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
which preserves zero objects, inflations, deflations, as well as exact sequences
1
1

1

This is equivalent to the a priori slightly stronger assumption of preserving pushouts along inflations and pullbacks along deflations.
.

2.

A sub-exact structure of an exact
\infty
-category
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
consists of an exact
\infty
-category
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\ddagger},\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger})
, satisfying that
\mathcal{C}_{\ddagger}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\dagger}
.

Sub-exact structures can be pulled back along exact functors as follows.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
G\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
be an exact functor between exact
\infty
-categories and
(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\ddagger},\mathcal{D}^{\ddagger})
a sub-exact structure of
(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
. Then there exists a sub-exact structure
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\ddagger},\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger})
of
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
, such that
G
defines an exact functor
G\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\ddagger},\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger})\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\ddagger},\mathcal{D}^{\ddagger})
.

Proof.

We set
\mathcal{C}_{\ddagger}\subset\mathcal{C}_{\dagger}
to be the subcategory of morphisms whose image under
G
lies in
\mathcal{D}_{\ddagger}
. We define
\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger}
similarly. It is straightforward to verify that
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\ddagger},\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger})
is an exact
\infty
-category and that
G\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\ddagger},\mathcal{C}^{\ddagger})\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\ddagger},\mathcal{D}^{\ddagger})
is an exact functor.
∎

Example 5.8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
are stable
\infty
-categories and
G\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
is an exact functor in the usual sense, i.e. a functor that preserves finite limits and colimits. Then
G
defines an exact functor
G\colon(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})\rightarrow(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D})
between exact
\infty
-categories. Applying
Lemma

5.7
, we obtain an additional exact structure
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
on
\mathcal{C}
by pulling back the split-exact structure
(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{D}_{\dagger},\mathcal{D}^{\dagger})
on
\mathcal{D}
. A fiber and cofiber sequence in
\mathcal{C}
is exact in
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
if and only if its image under
G
is split-exact.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
G\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
be an exact functor between stable
\infty
-categories. If
G
is a spherical functor, then the exact
\infty
-category
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
from
Example

5.8
is Frobenius. The subcategory of injective and projective objects of
\mathcal{C}
consists of the additive closure of the essential image of the right adjoint
H
of
G
.

Remark 5.10
.

A result similar to
Proposition

5.9
appears for triangulated categories in
[
BS21
]
, see Theorem 4.23 and Remark 4.24 in loc. cit.

Proof of
Proposition

5.9
.

Let
F
be the left adjoint of
G
and
H
the right adjoint of
G
. By the sphericalness of
G
, we have that
H\simeq F\circ T_{\mathcal{D}}
, with
T_{\mathcal{D}}
the cotwist functor of the adjunction
G\dashv H
, see
[
DKSS21
, Cor. 2.5.16]
. In particular, it follows that the essential images of
F
and
H
, and hence also their additive closures in
\mathcal{C}
, agree.

We begin by showing that every object of the form
F(d)\in\mathcal{C}
with
d\in\mathcal{D}
is injective. A dual argument shows that every object of the form
H(d)\in\mathcal{C}
is projective. Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
and consider an extension
\alpha\in\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(F(d),c)
. The extension
\alpha
corresponds to a fiber and cofiber sequence
c\rightarrow z\rightarrow F(d)
in
\mathcal{C}
. The image of this fiber and cofiber sequence under
G
splits (i.e. defines an exact sequence) if and only if
G(\alpha)\simeq 0
. The commutative diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(GF(d),G(c))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(F(d),c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(d,G(c))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

with
\operatorname{u}
the unit of
F\dashv G
, shows that
G(\alpha)\simeq 0
if and only if
\alpha\simeq 0
. This is the case, if and only if the fiber and cofiber sequence
c\rightarrow z\rightarrow F(d)
already splits. Since
c
and
\alpha
were chosen arbitrarily, it follows that
F(d)
is injective.

Next, we show that
\mathcal{C}
has enough projective objects. Let
c\in\mathcal{C}
. We have a fiber and cofiber sequence
c\xrightarrow{\operatorname{u}_{c}}HG(c)\rightarrow T_{\mathcal{C}}(c)
, where
\operatorname{u}_{c}
is a unit map and
T_{\mathcal{C}}
the twist functor of
G\dashv H
. We apply
G
and extend to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{\mathcal{D}}G(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GHG(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GT_{\mathcal{C}}(c)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\operatorname{u}_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{cu}_{G(c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\square}

with
\operatorname{cu}_{G(c)}
a counit map and all squares biCartesian. This shows that the sequence
G(c)\xrightarrow{G\operatorname{u}_{c}}GHG(x)\rightarrow GT_{\mathcal{C}}(c)
in
\mathcal{D}
splits and hence that that
c\xrightarrow{\operatorname{u}_{c}}HG(x)\rightarrow T_{\mathcal{C}}(c)
is exact. This shows that
\mathcal{C}
has enough projective objects.

Finally, we show that any injective or projective object lies in the additive closure (closure under taking direct sums of direct summands) of the essential image of
H
. Let
I
be injective. Then we have an exact sequence
I\rightarrow HG(I)\rightarrow T_{\mathcal{C}}(I)
in
\mathcal{C}
. Since
I
is injective, this sequence splits, showing that
I
is a direct summand of
HG(I)
, as desired. If
P
is projective, we similarly find an exact sequence
T_{\mathcal{C}}^{-1}(P)\rightarrow FG(P)\rightarrow P
, showing that
P
is a direct summand of
FG(P)\simeq HT_{\mathcal{D}}^{-1}G(P)
, as desired, concluding the proof.
∎

5.2
Extriangulated categories

Extriangulated categories were introduced by Nakaoka-Palu in
[
NP19
]
as a simultaneous generalization of triangulated and exact
1
-categories. An extriangulated category
(C,\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
consists of

•

an additive
1
-category
C
,

•

an additive bifunctor
\mathbb{E}\colon C^{\operatorname{op}}\times C\rightarrow\operatorname{Ab}
to the additive
1
-category of abelian groups, of which we think as describing an interesting class of extensions in
C
and

•

a sequence
\left(Y\rightarrow Z\rightarrow X\right)=\mathfrak{s}(\alpha)
, called realization, associated to each
\alpha\in\mathbb{E}(X,Y)
,

subject to number of conditions, see
[
NP19
]
. We will refer to the realization sequences as exact sequences in
C
, though Nakaoka-Palu call them conflation sequences.

Examples of extriangulated categories are, besides triangulated and exact categories, extension closed subcategories of triangulated categories. A further natural source of extriangulated categories are the homotopy
1
-categories of exact
\infty
-categories. To see this, note that by
[
Kle22
]
any exact
\infty
-category admits a stable hull, meaning that it can be embedded as an extension closed subcategory in a stable
\infty
-category. Passing to homotopy categories, one obtains an extriangulated structure on the homotopy category, as it is an extension closed subcategory of the triangulated homotopy category of the stable hull. An independent and more direct proof that the homotopy category is extriangulated also appears in
[
NP20
]
.

In the remainder of this article, we will assume all extriangulated categories to be
k
-linear, with
k
a field, meaning that the
1
-category
C
is
k
-linear and
\mathbb{E}
factors through
\operatorname{Vect}_{k}\to\operatorname{Ab}
.

Given a
k
-linear exact
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, the additive functor of extensions
\mathbb{E}\colon\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}_{k}
in the extriangulated structure of
\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{C}
describes isomorphism classes of exact sequences in
\mathcal{C}
. The functor
\mathbb{E}
can be useful for studying the exact
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
. For instance, one can use it to formulate a notion of cluster tilting object in the exact
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, and this is simply an object which becomes cluster tilting in the extriangulated homotopy category
\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{C}
in the sense of
Definition

5.15
below.

The definition of a Frobenius extriangulated category is analogous to the definition of a Frobenius exact
\infty
-category.

Definition 5.11
.

Let
(C,\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangualated category.

1)

An object
P\in C
is called projective if
\mathbb{E}(P,X)\simeq 0
for all
X\in C
and injective if
\mathbb{E}(X,P)\simeq 0
for all
X\in C
.

2)

We say that
C
has enough projectives if for each object
X\in C
there exists an exact sequence
X\rightarrow P\rightarrow Y
with
P
projective. Similarly, we say that
C
has enough injectives if for each object
Y\in C
there exists an exact sequence
Y\rightarrow I\rightarrow X
with
I
injective.

3)

We say that
C
is Frobenius if
C
has enough projectives and injectives and the classes of projective and injective objects coincide.

The inflations, deflations and exact sequences in an exact
\infty
-category can be read off from the extriangulated structure of its homotopy category. Similarly, the condition of being a projective or injective object in an exact
\infty
-category can be tested in the extriangulated homotopy category. We also get the following.

Lemma 5.12
.

A (
k
-linear) exact
\infty
-category is Frobenius if and only if its extriangulated homotopy category is Frobenius.

Remark 5.13
.

Given a Frobenius extriangulated category
C
, its stable category
\bar{C}
is defined as the quotient category by the ideal of morphisms which factor through an injective object. The stable category
\bar{C}
inherits the structure of a triangulated category, see Corollary 7.4 and Remark 7.5 in
[
NP19
]
.

If
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\dagger},\mathcal{C}^{\dagger})
is a (
k
-linear) Frobenius exact
\infty
-category, the triangulated homotopy category of its associated stable
\infty
-category
\bar{\mathcal{C}}
can be identified with the triangulated stable category
\overline{\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{C}}
of the Frobenius extriangulated homotopy category
\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{C}
.

Definition 5.14
.

An extriangulated category
(C,\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is called extrianguled
2
-Calabi–Yau if there exists an isomorphism of vector spaces
\mathbb{E}(X,Y)\simeq\mathbb{E}(Y,X)^{\ast}
, bifunctorial in
X
and
Y
.

We conclude this section with the definition of a cluster tilting object in an extriangulated category.

Definition 5.15
.

Let
T
be an object in an extriangulated category
(C,\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.

•

We denote by
\operatorname{Add}(T)\subset C
the additive closure of
T
, given by the subcategory given by objects equivalent to finite direct sums of direct summands of
T
.

•

T
is called basic if it can be decomposed into finitely many indecomposable direct summands which are pairwise non-isomorphic.

•

T
is called rigid if

\mathbb{E}(T,T)\simeq 0\,.

Further, a basic rigid object
T
is called maximal rigid, if there exist no object
X\in C
, satisfying that
T\oplus X
is rigid and
X\not\in\operatorname{Add}(T)
.

•

T
is called cluster tilting if it is basic rigid object and for every
X\in C
there exists an exact sequence
T_{1}\to T_{0}\to X
with
T_{0},T_{1}\in\operatorname{Add}(T)
.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
(C,\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a Frobenius extriangulated category with finite-dimensional Homs and
T\in C
a basic rigid object.

(1)

Then
T
is a cluster tilting object if and only if for every object
X\in C
the condition
\mathbb{E}(X,T)\simeq 0
implies that
X\in\operatorname{Add}(T)
.

(2)

Dually, the following two statements are equivalent:

•

For every
X\in C
there exists an exact sequence
X\to T_{0}\to T_{1}
with
T_{0},T_{1}\in\operatorname{Add}(T)
.

•

For every
X\in C
, the condition
\mathbb{E}(T,X)\simeq 0
implies that
X\in\operatorname{Add}(T)
.

Note that if
C
is extriangulated
2
-Calabi–Yau, this shows that
T
is a cluster tilting object if and only if the equivalent conditions of (2) hold.

Proof.

The proof of part (2) is analogous to the proof of part (1), we thus only prove part (1).

Assume that
T
is a cluster tilting object. Choose an exact sequence
T_{1}\to T_{0}\to X
in
C
with
T_{0},T_{1}\in\operatorname{Add}(T)
. The exact sequence of vector spaces, see
[
GNP21
, Thm. 3.5]
,

\operatorname{Hom}(T_{0},T)\to\operatorname{Hom}(T_{1},T)\to\mathbb{E}(X,T)\to\underbrace{\mathbb{E}(T_{0},T)}_{\simeq 0}

shows that if
\mathbb{E}(X,T)\simeq 0
, then the sequence
T_{1}\to T_{0}\to X
splits (since the connecting homomorphism vanishes), so that
X
is a direct summand of
T_{0}
.

For the converse direction, assume that for all
X\in C
, the condition
\mathbb{E}(X,T)\simeq 0
implies that
X\in\operatorname{Add}(T)
. Since
C
is Frobenius, we can find a deflation
P\to X
with
P
projective. We define
T_{0}=P\oplus(T\otimes\operatorname{Hom}_{C}(T,X))\in\operatorname{Add}(T)
. The apparent morphisms
T_{0}\to X
is again a deflation by
[
NP19
, Cor. 3.16]
. It remains to show that the term
T_{0}
appearing in the exact sequence
T_{1}\to T_{0}\to X
lies in
\operatorname{Add}(T)
. We have an exact sequence of vector spaces

\operatorname{Hom}(T,T_{0})\to\operatorname{Hom}(T,X)\to\mathbb{E}(T,T_{1})\to\underbrace{\mathbb{E}(T,T_{0})}_{\simeq 0}

see again
[
GNP21
, Thm. 3.5]
. The first morphisms is surjective by the definition of
T_{0}
, which shows that
\mathbb{E}(T,T_{1})\simeq 0
. It follows that
T_{1}\in\operatorname{Add}(T)
, concluding the proof.
∎

5.3
Exact structures via relative Calabi–Yau structures

Let
\kappa=k
be a field or
\kappa=k[t_{2n}^{\pm}]
the commutative dg-algebra of graded Laurent polynomials over a field. We fix a functor
G\colon\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
between
\kappa
-linear smooth and proper
\infty
-categories, which is spherical and carries a right
n
-Calabi–Yau structure.

We begin this section by exhibiting a relative version of the triangulated
n
-Calabi–Yau property
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}(X,Y)\simeq\operatorname{Ext}^{n-i}(Y,X)^{\ast}
for
\mathcal{B}
, using the relative right Calabi–Yau structure of
\mathcal{B}
. This relative Calabi–Yau property then yields a
2
-Calabi–Yau property on the Frobenius extriangulated structure on
\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{B}
.

Definition 5.17
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}
be compact objects.

1.

We denote by
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(X,Y)\subset\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)\in\mathcal{D}(\kappa)
the maximal direct summand
2
2

2

Equivalently this is the kernel of (
10
) in the abelian homotopy
1
-category
\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{D}(\kappa)
.
satisfying that the composite with

G\colon\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{A}}(G(X),G(Y))

(10)

yields the zero morphism in
\mathcal{D}(\kappa)
. We call
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(X,Y)
the
Calabi–Yau morphism object
.

2.

We denote by
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{B}}^{i,\operatorname{CY}}(X,Y)\coloneqq\operatorname{H}_{0}\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(X,Y[i])\in\operatorname{N}(\operatorname{Vect}_{k})
the
k
-vector space of
Calabi–Yau extensions
.

Lemma 5.18
.

(1)

The Calabi–Yau morphism objects assemble into a functor

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\colon\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\kappa)\,.

There further exists a natural transformation
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
which at a point
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}
is given by the inclusion of the direct summand
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(X,Y)\subset\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)
.

(2)

The Calabi–Yau extensions form the maximal subfunctor

\operatorname{Ext}^{i,\operatorname{CY}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\subset\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\colon\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}\rightarrow\operatorname{N}(\operatorname{Vect}_{k})

satisfying that
\operatorname{Ext}^{i,\operatorname{CY}}_{\mathcal{B}}(G(\mathchar 45\relax),G(\mathchar 45\relax))\simeq 0
.

Proof.

Consider the mapping space functor
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\colon\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
and the functor
\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\colon\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
, obtained from composing
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
with the functor
\mathcal{S}\xrightarrow{\pi_{0}}N(\operatorname{Set})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
taking connected components. Given
X,Y\in\mathcal{B}
, we denote by
(\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}})^{\operatorname{CY}}(X,Y)\subset\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)
the subset of homotopy classes of morphisms, satisfying that their image under
G
is a zero morphism in
\mathcal{A}
. Since zero morphisms are closed under composition, we find that
(\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}})^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
defines a subfunctor of
\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)
. We define the Calabi–Yau mapping space functor
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\colon\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
as the pullback of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}})^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)}}

in
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}},\mathcal{S})
. Using the fully-faithfulness of the Yoneda embedding, we can define a functor
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\colon\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\kappa)
via

\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{D}(\kappa)}(C,\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax))\simeq\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax\otimes C,\mathchar 45\relax)\,.

The natural transformation
\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\subset\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
induces a natural transformation
\eta\colon\operatorname{Mor}^{\operatorname{CY}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
. Note that for any pair
(X,Y)\in\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}
, the natural transformation
\eta
evaluates on the
i
-th homology group as the inclusion

(\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}})^{\operatorname{CY}}(X[i],Y)\subset\pi_{0}\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(X[i],Y)\,.

(11)

Using that any object in
\mathcal{D}(\kappa)
is equivalent to its homology, which is the direct sum of suspensions of copies of
\kappa
, we find that the inclusion
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(X,Y)\subset\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)
of the Calabi–Yau morphism object evaluates on the
i
-th homology group to the inclusion (
11
). This implies that the functor
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
indeed describes the Calabi–Yau morphism objects and
\eta
evaluates pointwise to their inclusion. This shows part (1).

Part (2) follows from the fact that on
i
-th homology,
\eta
exhibits
\operatorname{Ext}^{i,\operatorname{CY}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
as the desired maximal subfunctor of
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax,\mathchar 45\relax)
, by the description in (
11
).
∎

Recall from
Definition

2.13
, that given
A\in\mathcal{D}(\kappa)
, we write
A^{*}=\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{D}(\kappa)}(A,\kappa)
. Given a
k
-vector space
B
, we write
B^{*}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Vect}_{k}}(B,k)
.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathcal{B}^{c}
be compact objects.

(1)

There exists an equivalence in
\mathcal{D}(\kappa)

\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(X,Y)\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(Y[-n],X)^{\ast}\,,

bifunctorial in
X
and
Y
.

(2)

There exists an equivalence in
N(\operatorname{Vect}_{k})

\operatorname{Ext}^{i,\operatorname{CY}}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)\simeq\operatorname{Ext}^{n-i,\operatorname{CY}}_{\mathcal{B}}(Y,X)^{\ast}\,,

bifunctorial in
X,Y
.

Part (2) of
Proposition

5.19
should be seen as a relative version of the triangulated
n
-Calabi–Yau property. We postpone the proof of
Proposition

5.19
to the end of this subsection.

Let
(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}_{\dagger},\mathcal{B}^{\dagger})
be the exact
\infty
-category obtained by pulling back the split-exact structure on
\mathcal{A}
along
G
, see
Example

5.8
. We denote by
(\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}},\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{B}}^{1,\operatorname{CY}},\mathfrak{s})
the arising extriangulated homotopy category. Here, we use that
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{B}}^{1,\operatorname{CY}}
describes the extensions in this extriangulated category, as follows from
Lemma

5.20
. To be precise, we also abuse notation by labeling by
\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{B}}^{1,\operatorname{CY}}
the second functor in the factorization

\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{B}}^{1,\operatorname{CY}}\colon\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}\longrightarrow N(\operatorname{Vect}_{k})\,.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}
. Consider a fiber and cofiber sequence
X\rightarrow Z\xrightarrow{\alpha}Y
in
\mathcal{B}
and let
\beta\colon X\rightarrow Y[1]
be the cofiber morphism of
\alpha
. Then
\beta
lies in
\operatorname{Ext}^{1,\operatorname{CY}}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)\subset\operatorname{Ext}^{1}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)
if and only if the image of the fiber and cofiber sequence under
G
splits.

Proof.

We show that the fiber and cofiber sequence
G(X)\rightarrow G(Z)\xrightarrow{G(\alpha)}G(Y)
splits if and only if the cofiber morphism
G(\beta)
vanishes. By definition, the latter is equivalent to
\beta
being a Calabi–Yau extension. The forward implication is clear. For the converse, suppose that
G(\beta)
is zero. Then its fiber morphism, given by
G(\alpha)
, is equivalent to
G(X)\oplus G(Y)\xrightarrow{(0,\operatorname{id})}G(Y)
. This shows that the fiber and cofiber sequence splits.
∎

Proposition 5.21
.

The extriangulated category
(\operatorname{ho}\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}},\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{B}}^{1,\operatorname{CY}},\mathfrak{s})
is Frobenius and extriangled
2
-Calabi–Yau. The exact
\infty
-category
(\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}},\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}^{\operatorname{c}},\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\dagger})
is hence also Frobenius.

Proof.

The Frobenius property is shown in
Proposition

5.9
. The statement about the
2
-Calabi–Yau property follows directly from
Proposition

5.19
.
∎

Remark 5.22
.

The proof of
Proposition

5.9
shows that the suspension functor of the stable
\infty
-category
\bar{\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}}
arising from
(\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}},\mathcal{B}_{\dagger}^{\operatorname{c}},\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\dagger})
, see
Proposition

5.5
, acts on objects as the twist functor of the spherical adjunction
G\dashv H
, or by the relative right
2
-Calabi–Yau structure equivalently as the delooping (or negative shift) of the Serre functor of
\mathcal{B}
.

It is an interesting problem to determine whether
\bar{\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}}
inherits a right
2
-Calabi–Yau structure from the relative right
2
-Calabi–Yau structure of
\mathcal{B}
.

We conclude this section with the proof of
Proposition

5.19
.

Proof of
Proposition

5.19
.

Part (2) follows from part (1). We thus show part (1). Let
H
be the right adjoint of
G
and
\operatorname{u}\colon\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}\rightarrow HG
be the unit. Note that there is a commutative triangle as follows.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax_{1},\mathchar 45\relax_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{A}}(G(\mathchar 45\relax_{1}),G(\mathchar 45\relax_{2}))}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax_{1},HG(\mathchar 45\relax_{2}))}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{u}\circ\,\mathchar 45\relax}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

(12)

We thus have a commutative diagram in
\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c},\operatorname{op}}\times\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}},\mathcal{D}(\kappa))

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax_{1},\mathchar 45\relax_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax_{1},\mathchar 45\relax_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{u}\circ\,\mathchar 45\relax}

which induces by the relative Calabi–Yau structure a natural transformation

\xi\colon\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax_{1},\mathchar 45\relax_{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{fib}(\operatorname{u}\circ\,\mathchar 45\relax)\simeq\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax_{1},\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\ast}(\mathchar 45\relax_{2})[-n])\,.

Consider the natural transformation
\nu\colon\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax_{1},\mathchar 45\relax_{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\operatorname{CY}}(\mathchar 45\relax_{2}[-n],\mathchar 45\relax_{1})^{\ast}
appearing in the following commutative diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(\mathchar 45\relax_{1},\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}^{\ast}(\mathchar 45\relax_{2})[-n])}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

To prove part (1), we show that
\nu
is a natural equivalence, meaning that it evaluates at any pair
X,Y\in\mathcal{B}^{c}
to an equivalence. Consider the sequence
HG(Y)[-1]\rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(Y)[-n]\rightarrow Y\xrightarrow{\operatorname{u}}HG(Y)
in
\mathcal{B}^{\operatorname{c}}
, where any three consecutive terms form a fiber and cofiber sequence. Applying
\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,\mathchar 45\relax)
, we obtain the upper sequence in
\mathcal{D}(\kappa)
in the following diagram,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,HG(Y)[-1])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{B}}^{*}(Y)[-n])}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{Mor}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)}}
